Hailey Bieber’s It-Jacket Wardrobe Is Becoming Untenable

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Hailey Bieber must have some serious wardrobe storage. The Rhode founder has long been a devoted collector of excellent outerwear—oversized leather bombers, vintage furs, sculptural shearling, and the occasional extravagant Conner Ives robe—but her latest run of looks in New York has taken things to another level. At this point, we’re struggling to keep up with her ever-expanding collection of It-jackets.

The last few days have seen Bieber leave just about everybody else out in the cold. On November 17, she stepped out a Phoebe Philo shaggy Dancer jacket: cropped, voluminous, in mottled brown and gray shearling. She paired it with her trusty light-wash Levi’s Ribcage jeans, vintage black Manolo Blahnik sandals, and amber-tinted Gucci sunglasses. The jacket, now sold out, was the undisputed star of the outfit. It’s a tried-and-true Bieber styling trick and very much a formula to follow this season.

The following day, she dipped into Carolyn Bessette-Kennedy’s wardrobe playbook for an appearance at the New York Times DealBook Summit. Bieber wore a black Yohji Yamamoto jacket from spring 1998, paired with a white pencil skirt and black pumps. The same design was famously worn by Bessette-Kennedyin 1998. It was a major archival find.

But she wasn’t finished. Later, Bieber emerged in an ivory satin opera coat by New York-based Oriori Maison, framed by a dramatic swathe of voluminous gray-white fur, worn with black trousers, dark sunglasses, and her trusty Inez pumps from Reformation.

The designer behind the coat, Xiaoxi Zhang, tells Vogue that Bieber’s stylist contacted her directly to arrange the pull. For Zhang, whose designs explore Chinese heritage through a contemporary wardrobe, the moment is particularly meaningful. “I’ve always loved my cultural heritage, but I’ve never really worn traditional Chinese clothing,” Zhang shared. “Before making my first Chinese-inspired jacket, my intention was always to find a way to bring elements of Chinese culture into everyday fashion—to make heritage feel natural, modern, and wearable.”

Zhang made her first jacket in 2024, initially just for herself, rather than as a business venture. Discovering vintage quilts helped crystallize her approach. “It was the beginning of seeing my culture through my own perspective, rather than simply recreating the traditional,” she says. Her luxurious satin-and-fur creation has now found its way onto one of fashion’s most closely watched dressers. Her one-of-one pieces using sumptuous archival textiles are dropped via Instagram, if you want to emulate this excellent look.

By November 19, Bieber was back in black leather, arriving at Rigor Hill Market in a glossy, longline leather coat, relaxed trousers, and black shoes worn with white socks.

Of course, Bieber’s expansive love of leather outerwear is nothing new. Earlier this fall, she was spotted in a fringed leather blazer, while her collection of oversized bombers and sharply tailored jackets has long been the foundation of her off-duty wardrobe. What’s changed is the sheer variety: from minimalist archival tailoring to flamboyant, fur-trimmed opera coats, Bieber is evidently having fun with her outerwear, heralding a post-quiet luxury era that keeps things cozy.

And she’s certainly not alone. As Vogue recently observed, the personality coat is having a major moment, with Jennifer Lawrence embracing Colleen Allen’s chartreuse velvet housecoat, and other fashion favorites turning to extravagant silhouettes and trimmings.

If there’s one thing we’ve learned from Hailey Bieber’s fall wardrobe, it’s that you can never have too many great jackets. Though, at this rate, she may need another home to house them all.

‘Project Hail Mary’ Almost Had a Completely Different Ending for Rocky

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

In a historic move, actor and puppeteer James Ortiz is eligible for a Best Supporting Actor nomination at the 99th Academy Awards for his standout performance on Project Hail Mary. He’s the mind and movement behind the blockbuster’s lovable alien creature, Rocky, who led a team of puppeteers on set, essentially performing “Waiting for Godot in space” opposite Ryan Gosling with take after take of improvisation.

From filmmakers Phil Lord and Chris Miller, Project Hail Mary took audiences out of this world in an Andy Weir adaptation, penned by Drew Goddard, that wasn’t just visually stunning, but was a surprisingly moving two-hander between a man way in over his head and a creature made of rock, both on a dangerous mission to save their home planets.

At this year’s SCAD Savannah Film Festival, Collider’s Steve Weintraub had the pleasure of moderating an in-depth Q&A with Ortiz to discuss the artistry of bringing one of 2026’s most memorable characters to life. Alongside Gosling’s Ryland Grace, Rocky becomes Project Hail Mary’s beating heart, and during this conversation, Ortiz shares the behind-the-scenes work it took to bring them from the page to the screen. We learn all about the practical puppetry, the limitations that allowed their creativity to soar, how Rocky’s most iconic moments and quips came to be. Don't miss the full Q&A in the video above, with a transcript provided below.

COLLIDER: Let's talk a little bit about your background and how you got involved in the project.

JAMES ORTIZ: So I was cast as an actor a thousand years ago by the casting director, Jeanne McCarthy. You may have seen her name at the end of this film. I was cast in a pilot that no one ever saw because it never aired. It was for a network that we don't have to mention.

It was a great experience, but shortly after that, in New York, where I'm based, I was developing this sort of side career that turned into my full career, which is puppeteering and designing puppets for the theater. It kind of became this funny opportunity for me to play characters against my type. I could play things that I would never, ever be cast as because not every day you need, like, I don't know, a gay mad scientist. So, sometimes you need other flavors, and it was so exciting to be able to do that.

So Jeanne remembered that I existed, and she would bring me in for stuff, but oftentimes I would go, "Oh, I can't come in because I have this puppet thing." And she would go, "Puppets?" And long story short, she gave me a phone call. She told me about this story, and I went, "This sounds incredible, but do they really mean a puppet? Do they mean that?" And I had a great meeting with Phil [Lord] and Chris [Miller]. It was love at first sight. We absolutely adore each other. And then we were off to the races, and I was on set trying to make a faceless five-legged creature lovable.

If Rocky could watch Project Hail Mary, what would he complain the filmmakers got wrong about him?

ORTIZ: I think Rocky doesn't think that they're funny, so I think Rocky would hear everybody laughing and go, "Not funny, not funny." I think that'd probably be the biggest thing.

If you could sit down with Jim Henson, what would you want to talk about?

ORTIZ: I don't know if I would say anything. I would just sort of sit there like a loser. I've been really fortunate; I've met a lot of his children because they run different puppet organizations all over the country, and it's been really, really special. I did a show called The Woodsman a thousand years ago in New York, and it was really, really special. Cheryl Henson moderated a talk-back very much like this, and she told me very effusively how much her father loved the Oz stories, and it was really meaningful to hear that.

How many voicemails have you recorded for friends and family as Rocky?

ORTIZ: The limit does not exist. I couldn't even begin to give you a number.

How do you feel about doing the voice in public, like in moments like this?

ORTIZ: Yeah. What are we trying to communicate?

I'm sure there are some people in the audience that would like a voicemail from Rocky.

ORTIZ: I think what it would probably be would be, "You reach voicemail of this human. Make no sense. Leave message after beep. Beep now. It's time for a beep. Where is beep, question? Make no sense. Make no sense. Uh, uh, uh." And then it gets cut off, because I don't think Rocky fully understands what's happening.

When was the first time you said, "Fist my bump," and did you realize what it was going to become?

ORTIZ: I had no idea. We worked on the movie. We worked so hard, and I left, and I had such a great experience. And then I really had no sense that anybody would like it, frankly. That's maybe a failing of mine, but I've been around long enough to know that even if everybody that worked on it did everything right, it doesn't always translate. It doesn't always land, and the fact that it has is pretty, pretty wild. So, I didn't really think that far ahead, to be honest.

Frankly, also, I was only meant to be the person on set to help Ryan [Gosling], to help the DP. I was the steward of the character. I was just borrowing Rocky. And the fact that they lent him fully to me towards the end is still something I'm going to be processing for the rest of my life.

I'm going to dig down a little bit, but do you remember when you first said, "Fist my bump?"

ORTIZ: It had to have been in one of the scenes. It probably was in an improv that we were just kind of going. I was working so hard on just trying to make Rocky as truthful as possible that sometimes I would forget that there were catchphrases I had to hit. So most of the way that we were shooting is that Phil and Chris had an earpiece in my ear, and they could offer thoughts in between, in takes. So sometimes they would go, "Say fist my bump," and you're like, "Right. ‘Amaze, amaze, amaze,’” or whatever, right? So I can't actually tell you when I did it, but I know it would've been at some point then.

You said you and Ryan would sometimes improvise for incredibly long stretches. What's something Ryan did or said as Grace that completely caught you off guard, and how did you respond as Rocky?

ORTIZ: [Laughs] The only thing that's coming to mind right now is because when we shot it, remember that meme that went around three years ago that was “very demure?” There was one day where we were just going and riffing and riffing and going, and at some point, we were talking about Rocky or something, and he went, "It's very demure." And you saw him in real-time go, "No,” and try to backpedal. And in real-time, I think I went, "What is demure, question?"

We just kind of would go. We would just waste camera time.

We're at SCAD, and there are a lot of students in the audience, and I am 100% sure there are a few people out there that would love to do what you do. What advice would you like to share? And part two is, what advice were you given that was absolute horseshit?

ORTIZ: I may have to curse to answer this question, too. I was told by an acting professor a long time ago, "This is one motherfucker of a business." And, yes, it's hard, but I don't think that means it's bad. I think the most rewarding things have been the things that have been hard-won. That's what I would say.

But I would also say about the first question, if you want to play characters that are not easy to define, act more. Act all the time. Try to get opportunities to play characters that you wouldn't be cast as. Read roles that you're like, "I don't think this is for me. Let's try." And also, I think puppetry is an inherently physical art form, so I would also say invest in using your body to play the character.

Something that I've been saying endlessly all over this process has been saying that Rocky's movement is actually Rocky's subtext. The thing that Rocky is feeling is actually what is coming out in his body. The thing that he's saying is the best the translator can manage. So, that was something that I was always tracking: What is Rocky feeling at any given moment? Even though I don't know if Rocky has a deep sense of that themself. They don't. And over the course of the story, we get to see that Rocky actually learns what feeling is through Grace. So, that was another whole evolution.

But I guess the answer is movement theater, playing wacky parts, and letting your imagination not be limited.

I would also say, just from my perspective, I don't think people realize you really have to work your ass off to do something. You've got to give almost everything of yourself to something that you love to be successful.

ORTIZ: Yeah. I think that's completely right. I knew when I got the job that it wasn't going to be me at the end, but what does that matter? My job was to be of service. My job was to make the film work. My job was to make this character that is totally untenable — five legs, no face, speaks in this way, comes from a culture that is unimaginable — and I just went, "I want to always deliver something that feels consistent, that feels alive, that feels surprising, and I want to help Greig Fraser, who's shooting this movie, who has to emotionally find where the camera should go, and emotionally help Ryan in these long scenes where he has so much to do." Can you imagine him trying to do this to a tennis ball? It would've been a nightmare.

So, it was always the objective of just going all the way, and I think I have a “too much” gene as it is, so I couldn't help it.

Was there a moment during filming when you realized Rocky had stopped being a puppet and had become a real character in everyone's eyes?

ORTIZ: Everybody was talking to Rocky and not me. [Laughs] It was exactly that.

The first few weeks of the process, we were still finding our sea legs and figuring some stuff out. Phil gave me some great advice. We shot a scene that actually didn't make the cut — it's on the deleted scenes. My first day of shooting was this scene where we were in these two bunk beds. It was a great scene, and I loved doing it, but you could feel everybody on set who might've been skeptical that this would work, you could feel every crew member's shoulders just relax and lean in.

But Phil pulled me into his little tent with all my other Rocketeers, and we looked at some of the footage from that day, and he was like, "I think you can trust stillness more," because I was overcomplicating the fact that he doesn't have a face means you have to communicate through movement. But I think I was doing too much, and he was like, "Remember, this is in IMAX, so you can't be like [thrashes around] the entire time.”

So, it was a really cool lesson. It was a little bit like going to the Michael Caine school of acting, of never blink, always look at that side of the camera, not that. It was all that stuff that I was learning on a creature with no face.

By the way, that advice that Michael Caine said about not blinking is dead-accurate.

If any of you are acting students, I swear to you, you've got to learn how to not blink, and if you look at people's performances, like great actors, you will notice they do not blink. They're very specific when they blink because they feel that it's like a character moment.

I swear it's a little thing that you should absolutely practice.

ORTIZ: There's footage you can find of Michael Caine explaining this. Do it. It's so valuable because it's about using the medium. Theater people know how to use their whole body, and a lot of film acting is like, "This is now your proscenium. What are you going to do? How do you use it?" And, yeah, that's always the exercise.

Let's talk a little bit about what the physical rig was like. How many people does it take to operate Rocky, and how do you keep the team in sync?

ORTIZ: Great question. So it was me — I was basically always on the carapace, the main body. There were handles coming out of his mid-back, sort of like a fighter pilot, and that was how I could animate the movement of the head. There were sometimes three to seven people operating his other appendages, and I was always sort of leading the charge on what that was.

So we spent eight weeks basically learning how to work together as kind of a company. But the thing that would keep us tethered together, because puppetry can inherently be very technical — I promised Ryan the first day that we started talking about this project, and I had already gotten the part, I did say, "Listen, puppetry can be technical, but I guarantee you can improv with me, and I'm not going to leave you hanging, and I'm not going to let the technical side win." Right? It has to be an emotional performance.

And sometimes when I could feel my other performers trying to do one thing with a leg, and it wasn't going to happen, and they were getting frustrated, all of that, I would just say, "Let's not try to aim for perfection. Let's just focus on the feeling." So sometimes we would finish a take, and I would go, "That was awesome. Let's try it again, and this time it's Christmas morning, and you're tiptoeing down the stairs, and you're so excited." That has nothing to do with the scene, but that's the emotional beat that we were trying to sort of land.

So, that would be the one way, especially when we would be improvising and had to just lean in.

Was there ever a scene where the limitations of the puppet made the performance better?

ORTIZ: [Thumbs down] So, I think it's been well documented that basically they were like, "So he's going to do a thumbs up," and I was like [shakes head], because Rocky's elbows don't rotate. Rocky doesn't have wrists, which was something I would say all the time, and they'd go, "What does that mean?" And I was like, "Well, you can't do a thumbs up without a wrist." And we would try to do his finger, but that looked like something else. That looked like, "One minute!" So that didn't work.

That was something that we sort of showed on the day, and it was something that one of my puppeteers was like, "Wouldn't it be funny if he goes, 'Thumbs up?'" [Thumbs down] And it is! So that was one sort of happy accident, as Bob Ross would say.

Phil and Chris are known for finding movies in the editing room. Did they do anything with the editing that really surprised you about Rocky's performance?

ORTIZ: Yes. What I loved about working with them is that, again, I was a theater person from nowhere. The theater credits don't always transfer in the world of movies, and they would shoot every scene until basically everybody's ideas had been wrung out. It wasn't just like, "Let's do the scene on paper." Which, by the way, Drew Goddard wrote an incredible script, and we would do that, and then Ryan would have a cool idea, or I would have a cool idea, or Phil and Chris would go, "We were thinking it would be fun if..." And we would just shoot. Sometimes it would be a three-line scene, and we would shoot a take for 45 minutes, going back and going back, and, “Try it again, and add this.”

So, we had so much material that frankly, I left every day going, "I have no idea what we just did." I completely blacked out. I was just trying to stay in the scene and trying to stay alive. So when I saw different cuts of the movie, they in fact were very different films from my estimation. The way that the scenes would be juxtaposed next to each other meant something else.

When I saw the final cut, I actually did go, "I think I want to try to get into the ADR booth and maybe try to complete some of Rocky's arcs a little bit more." Because we didn't have the real estate. The movie is from Ryan's perspective. Literally, you're inside of his head most of the time. When he realizes that Rocky speaks that way, we suddenly can see in Rocky Vision, right? So there wasn't a lot of real estate to put Rocky's arc in there.

The TLDR of what I'll just sort of say here is that we didn't have this moment where Rocky's sort of traumatic 80 years left alone on a spaceship was ever paid off. Through a series of just trial and error, we were able to find something, and I sort of raised my hand and said, "I think we need to change this line in this scene where I give him the astrophage.” And Rocky saying, "Grace say Grace will die, Rocky fix" was something that we added truly a month before the movie came out. And it was something that really made me so happy because, again, it's that moment when I was going, "I feel so much for this character. I really want to do him service." And we hadn't landed the plane on, well, where does that trauma go? We never addressed it. And I think the trauma has to turn into something positive. Rocky then takes the awful experience that they had, which we don't really talk about, and turns it into help for the person that he cares about the most.

So anyway, that's a roundabout way of answering that.

It has to be really crazy to be a part of a film like this that means so much to so many people. What has it been like meeting fans, and what do they always want to talk to you about?

ORTIZ: Oh, it's incredible. Like I was saying, we made this movie kind of in a bubble, and then it came out — and what we don't have time for is the time that I met Steve the first time. Do you want to do it? Can we do it?

This is your Q&A. You do whatever you want.

ORTIZ: Let's do this because I think it's real charming. So basically, we were doing a press junket in LA before the movie came out, and the only people that had seen the movie were press. We were doing this thing where they were being interviewed downstairs, and I was up with the Rocketeers doing basically a meet-and-greet with Rocky. So press was coming in. There were little bits of footage. The footage was still coming out. And it was good, but it was quick. It was two minutes with each person.

We should say the room that you were in was in a hotel, and there were curtains, so you could not see past the curtain to know what was inside. And the publicist would only say, "We have something we want you to do." We were not told what was inside that room. At least for me, they were like, "We just want you to go in the room."

ORTIZ: And basically, there were cameras set up that people could see Rocky because Rocky was on a table and alive, and, "Hello, friend!" And we would interact, and it would be a two-minute sit in the chair, take a picture, have a video, move on. And it had become kind of a little rote, and everybody was like, "I loved the movie," and that is kind of a kiss of death. We're like, "Did you?"

And this human walked in, the curtain parted, and Steve's face went [looks concerned]. And we were trying to engage, like, "Come over here and take a picture." He went [holds up hand and shakes head], and he just sort of moved towards us, and it was so moving. I told you this already. It was so moving because it was the first inkling I had that maybe this character worked because you were so affected. I had to break character. I was still puppeteering, and I went, "Steve, do you want to sit down?" It was so moving. We went long just to let you have your experience. It was really meaningful to me.

I'm very, very lucky with what I get to do, and as I told you backstage, I've interviewed Kermit and Miss Piggy. Literally, you just become a child. You do not see the puppeteers. You just see Kermit and Miss Piggy, and you just want to talk to them, and you completely forget about everybody else. I was just in London for something that I can't talk about, but they had, in this one room, the Rocky puppets. I was just there seeing them, and everyone was freaking out.

ORTIZ: That’s cool. That's nice to hear. Anyway, non-sequitur. I wanted to tell you that story because we had met before, and it was really meaningful to me. It was really meaningful, Steve.

Is there a deleted Rocky scene or an extended sequence that you think fans would absolutely lose their minds over if they saw?

ORTIZ: There's one little moment, and it wasn't even a deleted scene; it was sort of an addition to a scene. There's a scene when we are training to go fishing, and the actress who played the voice of Mary, Priya [Kansara], who's an incredible actress and was on set with us for only a few days. It was thrilling because Ryan and I had been shooting for five months, just me and him, basically doing Waiting for Godot in space, and then Priya would show up, and we had another scene partner, so we all just woke up in a fun way.

We had this funny idea that was maybe just for one or two takes total, where when Grace was sleeping, Mary and Rocky had been hanging out, and that Mary had taught Rocky knock-knock jokes. So in the middle of the scene, she goes, "Tell joke now." And then Rocky went, "Knock, knock." And then Grace went, "Ugh, who's there?" "Rocky!" And that was it. It went nowhere, and it didn't help anything, but it was a moment on set where we all kind of went, "Oh, this is silly. We're having a silly time."

What was the single most technically difficult Rocky scene to perform, and what's something audiences would never realize went into making those few seconds work?

ORTIZ: God, I don't know if there's one scene I could say that was really the hardest thing ever. What I would say is… I'll give you some wacky fun facts. So, every scene was a total challenge. Anytime that you see Rocky in camera, we were really there putting him there. The challenging part was making sure that we had enough space for the puppeteers, not Rocky, in every single take — and I mean shot, not scene. Every single shot there's a different use of it's CG, it's half CG, it's fully puppet, it's two-part puppets, whatever.

But I will say the scene where we're playing with the tape measure, the thing that thrills me beyond belief is that there's only one shot or two shots total in that when it gets cut back and forth that is CG. Everything else is us. I have to shout out Claire Roi Harvey, one of my puppeteers, and Fred [Buhagiar]. They were literally using these mechanical fingers to do this thing, and we would just cut and play, and it was the silliest day on set. It was so fun.

The hardest thing was the fact that they made Rocky out of fiberglass, which is what I asked for. It's a lightweight material. But all the Xenonite is made out of plexiglass. If you don't know, fiberglass will scratch the hell out of plexiglass, and you can't polish it. It's a streak, and it's ruined. So, we weren't allowed to touch our house because every time that we did, it would leave a God-awful gash, and then Greig didn't have anything to shoot through. So, they basically added layers of transparency the first few days that was protectant, but it made it more blurry, and then Greig was not happy, and then we had to go back and forth.

So there's only two shots in the movie — it's actually in the clock when Rocky pulls out the clock — that there's real glass that's actually there because real glass doesn't scratch the way that it does with fiberglass. So everything else is this bizarre thing where we weren't allowed to touch our home, which is challenging, as you can imagine.

Harry Kane shares update from hospital after undergoing ankle surgery

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Kane sustained a torn ligament in his left ankle in Bayern Munich’s draw with Cologne on Friday

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Harry Kane is targeting the “business end of the season” after undergoing surgery on a torn ligament in his left ankle.

Bayern Munich said the operation on the injury, sustained in Friday’s 1-1 draw with Cologne, was a success but the 33-year-old is expected to be out for months as a result.

Kane wrote on Instagram: “The highs and lows of life! Obviously very disappointed to get this injury but as always it’s an opportunity to show resilience and improvement going into 2027.

“I’ll be back fresh and ready to go for the business end of the season!

“Tough times don’t last, tough people do” – my guy @edelman11 (Julian Edelman, three-time Super Bowl winner with the New England Patriots) told me this one!

“Thank you for all the messages! Alles gut.”

Kane sustained the injury when he was scythed down in the area by Cologne defender Joel Schmied, who reached Michael Olise’s low cross first but clattered into the Ballon d’Or winner’s left leg in his follow-through at the back post.

The England captain writhed in pain and, although he gingerly got back to his feet and initially soldiered on, it was clear he could not continue and he hobbled off the field alongside two physios after treatment.

After the match Bayern confirmed Kane had torn the syndesmotic ligament and “will therefore be sidelined for the next few months”.

Kane earlier this month signed a new deal with the Bundesliga giants until June 2029 – reportedly worth in excess of £400,000 a week, making him the highest-paid player in Europe before bonuses.

Since arriving from Tottenham three years ago, Kane has bagged a scarcely believable 156 goals in 163 appearances in all competitions, helping Bayern win a league and cup double last season.

He went into the international break having a drawn a blank in his four previous matches for Bayern but he scored twice for England, including from the spot in their 3-2 Nations League win over Spain.

But after being forced off after 20 minutes at the Allianz Arena, replaced by Tom Bischof, Kane could now be facing a battle to be fully fit for England’s two-legged quarter-final against Norway in March.

Brazilian Court Orders Arrest of the Buenavista Over Corazul Dispute

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A Brazilian court ordered the arrest of Corazul’s the Buenavista at the Port of Santos after the cruise line’s exclusive Brazilian distributor claimed it was owed about $270,000 (R$1.48 million), according to a court decision dated Nov. 13.

Judge Frederico dos Santos Messias of the São Paulo State Court of Justice’s specialized maritime court granted the request from R11 Travel Viagens e Turismo against GLCL Global Cruiseline Ltd., the Cyprus-registered company that operates as Corazul.

The judge issued the order without first hearing from Corazul, the decision showed.

The order barred the Buenavista from leaving Brazilian waters. The court said the ship was anchored off Santos for repairs.

The judge set security at about $323,000 (R$1.78 million) and said the ship would be released if Corazul deposited that amount or posted a bank guarantee.

According to the decision, R11 said Corazul owed about $1.27 million (R$6.96 million) under a four-year exclusive distribution agreement signed in March. That figure included about $780,000 (EUR 675,000) in unpaid quarterly marketing contributions. After subtracting about $1 million (R$5.48 million) in passenger funds it held, R11 put the net balance at about $270,000.

The filing, as summarized by the court, described a series of disruptions including the cancellation of the Buenavista’s European season in June; the cancellation of its Oct. 23 inaugural Brazilian cruise nine days before departure and complaints about onboard service on the Oct. 30 sailing. The company has since suspended operations and R11 has stopped selling cruises on the ship.

R11 said Corazul demanded $400,000 on Nov. 4 before it would operate the Nov. 6 departure. R11 refused.

The court noted that only two of the 11 sailings scheduled between Oct. 23 and Nov. 27 operated. Corazul also suspended operations until Nov. 30 and moved the end of its Brazil season up to March 5, 2027. The judge wrote that Corazul’s own communications showed a “serious liquidity collapse.”

The dispute is headed to arbitration in São Paulo under AMCHAM, the American Chamber of Commerce for Brazil. The decision stated that R11 must file within 30 days or the order lapses. Corazul has five days after being served to contest it.

10 Crime Shows With the Greatest Villain Performances, Ranked

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

One of the greatest strengths of crime television is its ability to skew moral compasses and deliver fascinating, complex characters that challenge our understanding of humanity. This is true of detective drama and organized crime suspense, and it is particularly true of the many great villains that populate these series, whether they are psychotic serial killers defined by their sinister presence or oddly charismatic crooks who are as charming as they are chilling.

It goes without saying that the performances of such characters are instrumental in determining their quality. Everything from the subtleties of facial expressions to the rhythm of line delivery, and to the physicality they are presented with, is all key choices actors can make in defining the brand of villainy their character exudes. Whether viewers hold a wicked love for them or dread every second of screen time they receive, these crime series find astonishing highlights in the form of their biggest and baddest antagonists.

Despite only appearing in the third season of Boardwalk Empire, Gyp Rosetti (Bobby Cannavale) stands as one of the Prohibition-era gangster series’ most piercing and unforgettable characters. Within a story that often feels methodical and pragmatic, Rosetti injects a tense volatility into every single scene he appears in, which is a testament to the astonishing writing of the character and Cannavale’s note-perfect performance of such an explosive character.

Whereas so many of the other adversaries Nucky Thompson (Steve Buscemi) encounters attempt to outsmart him through business maneuvers or political plotting, Rosetti relies on ferocious, bloodthirsty brutality and unwavering intent. His destructive, overwhelming personality is only made all the more commanding by the character’s sensitivity to perceived slights, especially as Cannavale imbues every second of screen time he gets with the simmering suspense of a ticking time bomb. Whether he’s erupting into a fit of rage or hurling oddly elegant insults, Cannavale makes Rosetti one of the most intimidating and unpleasant TV antagonists of all time.

As a grim descent into the criminal underbelly of Gotham City, The Penguin marks one of HBO’s more compelling productions of the decade so far, a rich mixture of comic-book style and gangland brutality. Colin Farrell’s lead performance is a driving force of this, but the miniseries benefits from its vast array of striking characters, with Cristin Milioti’s turn as criminal heiress Sofia Falcone being the noteworthy standout as she blends an erratic, unpredictable ruthlessness with underlying vulnerability and sympathetic sorrow.

Milioti worked closely with both movement coach Julia Crockett and the production’s costuming department to perfect Sofia’s physicality. Through every movement, she embodies the volatility of her explosive aspirations fighting to break free from her past of repression and institutionalization. In isolation, she is exceptional — the fourth episode’s focus on her time in Arkham Asylum was instrumental in her Emmy Award win — but the crackling, high-octane tension she brings to scenes with Farrell is her true triumph, crafting a complex yet purposeful obstacle to Cobb’s own ambitions of controlling organized crime in Gotham.

Purely as a superhero villain, Wilson Fisk (Vincent D’Onofrio), otherwise known as Kingpin, is a perfect foil to Daredevil (Charlie Cox), with his brutish strength challenging the hero’s more precise and calculating fighting style. However, it isn’t the imposing physicality that makes Fisk such a great antagonist, but rather the depth, nuance, and tragedy D’Onofrio realizes in the character in addition to his intimidating frame.

He plays the part with a certain childlike vulnerability, a stiff social awkwardness that is almost endearing in tandem with Fisk’s sophistication and his love for Vanessa (Ayelet Zurer). His stammering, soft-spoken nature only makes his volatile rage all the more terrifying, grounding the character in his insecurities that can give way to a destructive wrath at any moment. D’Onofrio’s ability to navigate such a polarizing dichotomy is astonishing, bringing both the physical gravitas and the timid personality together to make for one of the best mob boss bad guys in the medium’s history.

It may be difficult to separate hero from villain in a series like The Sopranos. Everyone is bad. But some are worse than others, as Tony Soprano’s (James Gandolfini) rogues' gallery perfectly illustrates. Amid a collection of menacing mobsters, Joe Pantoliano’s Ralphie Cifaretto is a striking standout, with the duality Pantoliano brings to the role being a significant reason for this.

The character is both charming and reprehensible. He has an air of charisma and quick-witted comedy that creates some of the more memorable and wickedly hilarious scenes in the series, but his volatile nature also underlines some of the more disturbing moments as well. His abrupt, sadistic murder of Tracee (Ariel Kiley) is a frightfully disgusting scene that tends to linger in the minds of viewers, one only made more chilling by the way Pantoliano pivots straight back to Ralphie’s more cheerful demeanor almost immediately after. Even within Tony’s crew, he is a disconcerting, unpredictable presence. That he made such a visceral impression despite featuring in just 21 of The Sopranos’ 86 episodes is a testament to Pantoliano’s performance.

At its best, Killing Eve presents a mixture of high-octane spy thrills and international crime drama that is nothing short of addictive. Its two lead actresses are instrumental in creating this gripping atmosphere of anxiety and awe, with Jodie Comer utterly outstanding as the brutal yet brilliant international assassin, Villanelle. While the character is initially presented as a ferocious, psychotic killer, she gradually evolves as her complex cat-and-mouse obsession with MI5 agent Eve Polastri (Sandra Oh) grows increasingly intense and intimate.

Comer is brilliant at exploring the depths of Villanelle while still presenting her as a ruthless, unpredictable villain. Her ability to switch between accents, dialects, and even languages as Villanelle navigates the world of international crime is captivating, making for a frightfully capable antagonist who exudes a sense of magnetism with her mixture of callous practicality and sadistic ire. Even slight facial twitches become transfixing, whether she’s conveying boredom, curiosity, or menace. The end result is a bold villain laced with theatricality and intrigue who is able to be profoundly complex and absolutely sinister at once.

Four criminal masterminds. One of them was always going to be yours. Here is who comes for you, why they picked you out of everyone available, and what they want from the encounter.

Your nemesis does not want to beat you. He wants to know you completely, and he is patient enough to wait for it. The frightening part is how good the conversation is.

Your nemesis is not after money, territory or revenge. He is after a demonstration, and you are the person he has chosen to prove his point on.

Your nemesis respects you, which sounds flattering until you work out what it means. He chose you because nobody else was worth his time.

Your nemesis has no personal interest in you whatsoever, and that is precisely what makes him unsurvivable. You are not a rival. You are a stop on the way.

Justified becomes more than just a standard police procedural series off the back of its quirks and eccentricities. The setting, the style, and the tone all play a pivotal part in this, as do the characters themselves, with few being as defining as the overarching antagonist, Boyd Crowder (Walton Goggins). Crowder was originally set to die in the pilot episode, but Goggins’ infectious screen presence saw the writers extend his arc across the six-season stretch, with Boyd going from being a white supremacist to a born-again preacher with a penchant for organized crime.

The theatricality Goggins imbues in the part is transfixing. Lines aren’t simply recited; they are danced with, performed in the truest sense of the word. What could so easily have been a typical redneck villain is instead a captivating cacophony of Southern charm, intellectual curiosity, and treacherous, underlying vulnerability. He is the epitome of a villain you can’t help but love, a grandiose persona of mayhem whose chemistry with Timothy Olyphant’s U.S. Marshal Raylan Givens is an essential pillar of Justified’s arresting allure.

Sometimes, in great detective drama, the only thing more satisfying than seeing the hero solve the case is seeing them meet their match. Sherlock operates on this idea magnificently. Introduced in the Season 1 finale, Andrew Scott’s Jim Moriarty is the perfect foil to Sherlock’s (Benedict Cumberbatch) analytical expertise, and one that perfectly fits into the series’ modernizing take on Arthur Conan Doyle’s classic sleuth stories.

Instead of moustache-twirling vaudevillian villainy, Moriarty is a cold and calculating criminal mastermind who pushes Holmes to his limit and beyond. The theatricality and energized fun that Scott brings to the role is a huge factor as to why the character is so unforgettable, but his ruthless cunning is what establishes him in the viewer’s mind in the first place. Both within the context of the story—being the perfect adversary to Sherlock—and on his own merits—his mesmerizing mixture of twisted humor, arresting charisma, and genuine menace—Jim Moriarty is a true icon of television antagonism.

There are few who would dispute the notion that Dexter’s fourth season is its strongest. It benefits from unrelenting high-stakes tension, a number of devastating plot twists, and the impeccable performance of John Lithgow as the overarching antagonist, Arthur Mitchell. Also known as The Trinity Killer, Mitchell presents a chilling duality between suburban normality and murderous rage, and it’s a divide that Lithgow excels at exploring and blurring as his cat-and-mouse game with Dexter (Michael C. Hall) escalates throughout the season.

However, Mitchell isn’t presented merely as being a sadistic serial killer hiding in the cozy comfort of suburbia, but is rather dissected as a fascinating, often tragic man governed by an overbearing God-complex, festering trauma, and a deeply disturbed inner child. It is undeniably terrifying, but there is an acute pathos that viewers are compelled to feel for him as well. Complimented by his seismic impact on Dexter’s personal life, Arthur Mitchell is both a devastating villain and a bitterly complex soul, with Lithgow’s deft portrayal of him making for one of the best antagonists in crime television.

It is impossible to overstate the achievement of what Mads Mikkelsen achieved with his performance throughout Hannibal. Not only did he have to epitomize true, calculating evil in a series loaded with frightful antagonists, but he also had to inherit the role from Anthony Hopkins’ iconic, Academy Award-winning turn in The Silence of the Lambs. He excels with that same sense of refined, sophisticated menace, but he also finds room to impart his own nuances to the character.

Through his manipulative working relationship with traumatized FBI criminal profiler Will Graham (Hugh Dancy), Lecter connives to garner control while still satiating his own homicidal impulses. Mikkelsen approaches the character as a fallen angel, a devilish villain alien to human morality cloaked in a veneer of sharp professionalism. The only glimpses behind the façade, particularly early in the series, are subtle facial twitches and shifts in posture. It’s eerily graceful, completely engaging, and one of the best television performances of the 2010s.

Breaking Bad has no shortage of great villains, from the savagely unpredictable Tuco (Raymond Cruz) to the sadistic Todd (Jesse Plemons), but it is impossible to look beyond Giancarlo Esposito’s Gustavo Fring as being the series’ greatest antagonist. In contrast to the show’s many explosive and eccentric antagonists, Fring is an image of cold poise, a terrifying entity of restraint and discipline harboring a cunning, murderous mind beneath the surface of a fast-food restaurant chain owner.

With Esposito’s tight and tense performance capable of injecting both comedy and chilling suspense into scenes, Fring’s lack of theatricality becomes a spectacle in itself. This quiet nature is imbued with tremendous power courtesy of Esposito’s rhythmic yet calm line delivery and his icily still presence, both of which present him as a figure of ruthless efficiency. Whether Walter White (Bryan Cranston) is working under Fring or waging a deadly war of wits against him, the cartel drug lord exudes a frightful yet transfixing air of lethal composure that makes him the greatest crime TV villain of all time as well as one of the best characters the medium has ever seen.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Zcash Just Dropped Below $1,000 for the First Time Since September. Is This a Buy-the-Dip Opportunity?

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Most of its recent decline has been caused by a turbulent market, which probably won't be improving much in the very near term.

By Alex Carchidi – Nov 21, 2026 at 6:03AM EST

With Zcash (ZEC -7.27%) falling to $922 during its first big drop below $1,000 since September, it's natural for investors to wonder whether it's time to buy the dip, especially considering that the coin is still up 73% this year so far.

Price action alone doesn't make for a good investment thesis, so let's take a closer look at this privacy coin and determine whether it's worth buying right now, despite the turbulence and uncertainty in the crypto market and the world.

Zcash's recent decline from its highs is to be expected, given the bumps in the road it has faced.

For instance, on Oct. 29, hackers stole $6.3 billion from Binance, the largest crypto exchange, marking the largest-ever crypto theft and worsening the climate of fear that has been intensifying since a spate of other crypto hacks earlier in the year. Similarly, when October's Consumer Price Index (CPI) data came in above market forecasts, it sparked a flight from risky assets like crypto amid expectations that the Federal Reserve will hike interest rates at its next meeting in December. Separately, investors pulled $46 million from Grayscale's Zcash exchange-traded fund (ETF) in October, followed by another $15 million in early November.

None of those issues were caused by anything related to Zcash itself. Its main investment thesis, namely that investors will continue to seek to conceal their transactions and account balances from prying eyes, is still valid.

Now, let's turn to whether it makes sense to buy this coin right now.

Zcash just launched its latest network upgrade, NU7, which makes the chain significantly faster. It cuts the target block time, the gap between each batch of its confirmed transactions, from 75 seconds to 25 seconds. It also sequesters 60% of the network's transaction fees into a reserve that will disburse its funds to pay miners starting in around 2031. Those changes are well and good, but they don't imply any need to rush with a purchase.

One interesting factor that might make it a better buy is the spread of privacy in crypto. On Nov. 10, Helius, a Solana (SOL -4.23%) infrastructure company, launched a privacy protocol for use on Solana. Its CEO signaled the design was inspired by Zcash's. This imitation validates Zcash, but it also means more competition in the future.

Overall, I believe in Zcash's long-term investment potential, but the market is a bit too fragile for me to buy. A coin that lost over 41% in just eight weeks can do it again, and I do not want to be taking risks like that in the current environment of uncertainty.

But for those with a very high risk tolerance and a five-year-plus holding horizon, not to mention already having a diversified portfolio, buying a small amount of Zcash right now could still pay off.

Colson Whitehead's Brilliant Thriller Lands On Amazon's List Of The Best Books Of 2026

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

As 2026 draws to a close, many publishers and vendors are rounding up their list of the best books of 2026. It's no surprise that some incredible thriller books have landed on the Amazon Editor's list of the best books of 2026, including one of Colson Whitehead's most recent works.

The author of some of the greatest literature of the 21st century, readers who haven't yet delved into his expansive body of work are missing out. Whitehead's Pulitzer Prize-winning novel The Underground Railroad was adapted for the screen and is a perfect Prime Video miniseries for any viewer. His latest thriller is the conclusion of a trilogy Whitehead began in 2021, offering a stunning finale to a fun, atmospheric action-mystery series.

Whitehead is a two-time winner of the Pulitzer Prize, as his 2019 novel Nickel Boys was also awarded the top prize. The book was adapted into the critically acclaimed 2024 film of the same name. As one of the biggest book vendors of the modern era, the Amazon editors keep their fingers on the pulse not only of what's popular and attention-grabbing, but of landmark additions to the literary canon.

The third and final installment of Whitehead's Harlem Trilogy, Cool Machine, is number 10 on the Amazon list of 2026's best books. Featuring novels like American Hagwon by Min Jin Lee and Fruit Fly by Josh Silver, the list has a wide array of genres and novel types for readers of all kinds. However, thriller lovers will be glad to see that Cool Machine is featured prominently on the list.

Since 2016, some of the best thriller novels audiences have seen in years have come out, giving fans of the genre plenty to sink their teeth into.

The first two books in the series, Harlem Shuffle and Crook Manifesto, introduce the reader to Ray Carney, the compulsively compelling protagonist of the novels. Spanning the early 1960s through the '80s, the Harlem Trilogy is a vivid portrait of New York City during this period, digging into the historical events. The novel explores this through the lens of Ray's connection to the hidden underbelly of exciting heists and the people who want to control Harlem.

In Cool Machine, the reader picks up with Ray, his wife Elizabeth, and the future of his furniture business at the beginning of the significant economic and social changes of the 1980s. The changing landscape of New York is brought into sharp relief thanks to Whitehead's writing style and ability to craft characters the audience adores spending time with. Just as thrilling as his first two books in the series, without giving up the emotional poignancy Whitehead is known for, Cool Machine deserves its spot on the list of best books of 2026.

It doesn't come as a surprise that the Harlem Trilogy has been tapped for a screen adaptation at Apple TV+ (via Variety). Whitehead's work has been brought to life for both television and film, and his crystal-clear prose makes it easy to visualize the characters and settings of his stories. The Harlem Trilogy will make for a fantastic addition to Whitehead's filmography, as period pieces with elements of crime, especially heists, never go out of style.

Many readers, not just thriller fans, have found themselves falling in love with the Harlem Trilogy and blown away by the way Whitehead concludes the series in Cool Machine. Anyone who hasn't yet joined Ray on the streets of Harlem, or read Whitehead's work, should dive into these books as their next great read. Though 2026 isn't over yet, it's safe to say that Whitehead's Cool Machine will retain its spot as one of the best works of the year.

Cool Machine is available for purchase here.

Best Laptops for Travel (2026): Which to Take on the Road

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

As a laptop reviewer, I have plenty of devices to choose from when I travel for work. In some cases, my favorite overall laptop doesn’t translate into the one I want to travel with. When I’m squeezed into a plane, I’ll choose maximum battery life and portability over features like a bigger screen or more ports.

Fortunately, there are many great travel laptops, and you’ll still appreciate them when you aren’t on the road. Of the hundreds of laptops I’ve tested in my life, here are the ones I always choose to travel with.

If there’s an obvious pick for the best travel laptop, it’s the Dell XPS 13. It’s been the best compact laptop you can buy for years, and that’s even more true in its latest incarnation. The key dimension to pay attention to on the XPS 13 is the depth, which is crucial for air travel. At 7.9 inches, it’s among the smallest laptops you can buy. Those economy-class seat-back tray tables on airplanes are only around 10 inches deep, and while that’s technically enough to accommodate even a 16-inch MacBook Pro, whether you can actually open the lid and use the laptop is another question. The XPS 13 is even smaller than the MacBook Neo, despite having a larger screen.

The XPS 13 achieves this by using a 13.4-inch display (measured diagonally), compared to many of the 14-inch laptops on this list. Second, it has very narrow bezels around that screen, minimizing the device’s overall footprint. While 14-inch laptops have become more popular in recent years, that bigger display size also increases the overall footprint—and every millimeter counts when working on a plane. The XPS 13 still has enough room for your palms when typing (unless you have huge hands), and the keyboard is among my favorites to type on.

The other two major advantages for travel are weight and battery life. This little laptop only weighs 2.2 pounds, a full half-pound lighter than the 13-inch MacBook Air. You might forget you have it in your backpack while traveling.

Now, the XPS 13 isn't the most powerful laptop in the world—at least not the cheaper models. The standard version comes with only 8 GB of RAM and an Intel chip with only two Performance cores. It’s good enough for basic web tasks, but for real work, you might run into limitations. You can address this by opting for the 16 GB model (which keeps the price under $1,000) or upgrading to the much more powerful Core Ultra 7 355 processor. The latter is much more expensive and comes with twice the storage, but it doesn’t add extra weight or size. Regardless of the chip you choose, the XPS 13 gets absolutely fantastic battery life. It depends on what you're doing with it, but you could easily work through a long international flight without worrying about plugging in.

If weight is your primary concern, the Asus Zenbook A14 is another contender. Despite being larger overall than the XPS 13 (with a bigger screen), the Zenbook A14 weighs 2.16 pounds—a hair lighter than the Dell. That’s seriously impressive, and I still remember how astounded I was when I first picked it up. Asus debuts its “ceraluminum” material in the chassis, which gives you the sturdiness of aluminum with the lightness of plastic. It’s not quite as rigid as metal, but it’s not as flimsy as ultra-light laptops in the past.

If you’re less worried about working on the plane and more about lugging a laptop around, the Zenbook A14 is a good choice. The Zenbook A14 has several advantages over the XPS 13 that can be helpful on the go, including built-in ports like a headphone jack, USB-A, and HDMI. If you’re working out of a remote office or presenting to a client, these can definitely come in handy. The XPS 13 only has two USB-C ports and nothing else.

There are a number of different versions of the Asus Zenbook A14 floating around out there. I’d stay away from the 8 GB model Asus sells and opt for the 16 GB model instead. There’s also a newer model that uses the Snapdragon X2 Elite chip, but I don’t think it’s worth the extra $450—especially not for a laptop focused on travel.

The 13-inch MacBook Air also makes a great travel laptop. It’s relatively small, thanks to its 13.6-inch screen.

In terms of portability, its main advantage over the XPS 13 and Zenbook A14 is its thinness. At 0.42 inches, it’s the thinnest laptop out there—and it doesn’t stick huge rubber feet on the bottom to compensate like other laptops do, such as the Razer Blade 14. You might not think thickness matters much in a travel laptop, but if you’re squeezing stuff into a backpack, every extra millimeter matters.

The MacBook Neo is also worth mentioning here. It’s not quite as thin as the MacBook Air, but because its screen is 0.4 inches smaller, it has a more compact overall footprint. Battery life is the bigger issue when taking the Neo on a trip, as it doesn’t last as long as the MacBook Air or Dell XPS 13. The 8 GB of RAM limitation doesn’t affect its use as a travel laptop much, but it's something to consider for a work laptop in general. That said, it has a very pretty screen for a laptop at this price.

Budget laptops aren’t typically known for being particularly lightweight and travel-worthy. Most of them struggle, especially with battery life. The HP OmniBook 5 might be an exception. You can usually find it on sale for under $700, even with 16 GB of RAM and an OLED display.

The OmniBook 5 isn’t quite as compact as the MacBook Neo because of its larger screen, but it is also half an inch thick and weighs only 2.8 pounds. The Snapdragon X Plus also delivers solid battery life, plenty to feel comfortable traveling with. It’s not a laptop without compromises (like the poor touchpad), but it comes with 16 GB of RAM, unlike some other laptops at this price. That’s a solid deal if you need something to travel with on a budget.

Good question. Tablets can make for great travel devices because of their size and ease of use. If you need a proper keyboard and touchpad—and the app support to handle a typical workday—you’re probably better off getting a laptop.

The Surface Pro 12 is the two-in-one laptop I’d recommend first. It’s absolutely tiny for a computer, even though it runs the full version of Windows 11. Even with the detachable keyboard connected, it weighs only 1.8 pounds. The only real problem with bringing this on a plane is the kickstand, which adds some extra depth. It’s just small enough that it should fit while open on an airplane tray table, unlike the 13.8-inch Surface Pro model.

Of course, that kickstand is also a huge advantage for the Surface Pro 12 if you’re using it without the keyboard. While that would make work difficult, it’s ideal for watching movies. Set this up on your tray table with some movies downloaded, and you’ll be the envy of your aisle. And when you need to get some work done in a coffee shop or at a smaller table, you’ll be happy to have access to the keyboard and touchpad—both of which are very comfortable to use. Just know that it's sold separately.

The iPad Air, paired with the Magic Keyboard, avoids the Surface Pro 12’s kickstand issue. And with only an 11-inch screen, it’s perfect for use in small spaces—with or without the keyboard (note that there is also a 13-inch version). If you can accomplish all your work on an iPad, more power to ya! The iPad Air should give you everything you need, so long as you get the Magic Keyboard. If your travels involve more leisurely activities, the iPad Air is a great device for reading by the pool or for playing games.

One benefit of this latest version of the iPad Air is not only its performance (thanks to the M4 chip inside) but also the front-facing camera, which has been moved to landscape for more natural-feeling video calls. Of course, you could also opt for the 11-inch iPad Pro for extra performance (and the incredible tandem OLED screen). It’s also slightly thinner and lighter, though I don't think it's worth the significant price difference.

Lastly, there’s definitely room here for laptops with 360-degree hinges—a different class of two-in-one laptops. I haven’t tested a recent Windows device with this form factor that I truly loved, but there’s no question it works well on an airplane. Just fold the screen back with the keyboard face down, and you have the perfect screen for watching movies. The Lenovo Yoga 9i 14 is a gorgeous option, though it’s incredibly expensive right now. But if you can afford it, you’ll love the 2-in-1 action combined with one of the slickest designs imaginable. The Framework Laptop 12 is also worth considering. While it’s a bit thick and heavy for a travel laptop, its smaller screen, touchscreen, and 360-degree hinge make it a good option. It’s also one of the cheaper options available, and it’s user-upgradeable.

Whether you’re packing for a trip across the world or across the state, a proper travel laptop shouldn’t be heavy or overly large. That rules out 15-inch and 16-inch laptops, which are so big you can’t use them on airplane tray tables or at coffee shops. But even when you narrow it down to 14-inch laptops or smaller, there are three portability factors to consider.

When you’re on the go, you want a laptop that doesn’t constantly make you worry about finding an outlet. Bringing a laptop power bank can help, but the biggest factor in staying sane while traveling is a device that can stay on. Many factors affect how long a laptop can last, including CPU efficiency, battery wattage, screen brightness and resolution, and more.

Long-lasting laptops, such as the Dell XPS 13, will get you through a long flight. The processor is the main thing to look out for. Qualcomm’s Snapdragon X series of chips are battery-efficient, as are Apple’s M-series chips. Intel also has some highly efficient CPUs in its most recent lineup, though you’ll need to pay close attention to the model number, as it can vary wildly depending on the specific CPU you’re using. To dig deeper, read my extensive guide on How to Buy the Right Laptop, which breaks down different CPU options.

The size of seat-back tray tables varies. There’s no exact size because of various factors, such as the airline, aircraft, cabin, and seat. Remember: Just because a laptop can physically fit on a tray table doesn’t mean you’ll have space to open and use it. With that in mind, here are the general averages to keep in mind. Airlines do not list tray table sizes, but you can expect one in economy to measure around 16 x 9 inches, while premium cabins will have larger tables depending on the seat.

I’ve included both tablets and laptops in my recommendations, but your choice will depend on why you’re traveling with a device. If it’s for a vacation and you really only need a device to read, watch, and play on (perhaps with the occasional email check), a tablet works perfectly. Lounging with a tablet, whether on a plane or by the pool, feels much more leisurely than using a laptop. More importantly, it'll help you avoid dipping back into work mode. Whether an Android tablet, iPad, or Windows tablet, just pair it with a compatible keyboard case—and you’re good to go.

However, if you’re traveling for work (or need to work while traveling), a proper laptop will be better. The one you already have may work just fine, but frequent fliers know they prefer a more compact device. And more specifically, a small laptop with a keyboard and trackpad will work better on your lap or on small desks than a tablet.

Can Washington steal the NFC East crown? Hogs Haven readers weigh in after Thursday’s in over the Giants - poll results

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Here's what Ben Standig had to say earlier this week:

The 2026 NFL regular season is past the halfway point, but the finish line remains too far away to make sweeping declarations about how the NFC East race will unfold.

Suggesting that the eventual division winner will have at least eight victories doesn't seem like a stretch, in theory.

There's a solid argument that the Washington Commanders are the best all-around team among the remaining contenders. (Step aside, Eagles.)

When Washington sat at 1-3 following a dismal overseas loss to Indianapolis in Week 4, talk of an NFC East title felt like pure fantasy. Yet six games later, the burgundy & gold have quietly stitched together a 3-2-1 stretch highlighted by Thursday night's 24-21 win over the New York Giants at MetLife Stadium that has breathed a sense of renewed optimism into at least part of the fan base.

Now at 3-5-1, Washington still occupies third place in the division, trailing New York by 2 games. Even so, the underlying progress is real. Quarterback Jayden Daniels engineered a clutch late drive last Thursday night — connecting with Stefon Diggs on a 32-yard touchdown with 1:05 remaining — while Washington converted 7 of 14 third downs and held New York to 4-of-12 on third down.

Moreover, over the past 6 games, the Commanders have largely protected the football — carrying a +7 turnover differential on the season while leaning on young contributors like tight end Ben Sinnott, wideout Antonio Williams, and cornerbacks Trey Amos and (the not-so-young) Amik Robertson.

In this week's Hogs Haven Reacts survey, we asked readers two questions to gauge the community's temperature entering Week 11. The first was our weekly benchmark: Are you confident in the overall direction of the team?

This week, 46% of respondents answered "Yes," while 54% voted "No".

That 32-point surge from last week's 14% approval mark mirrors the 21-point jump we saw in Week 9 after Washington beat the Eagles. Nothing cures midweek angst quite like beating a division rival in prime time!

Still, the fact that 54% of voters remain in the "No" camp shows that skepticism runs deep. As several commenters and fans have mentioned, Washington has yet to beat a clear playoff contender outside of Seattle in Week 3, and they let winnable games against the Colts and Giants slip away earlier in the fall. Until the Commanders can achieve sustained winning, a majority of the fan base is keeping its guard up, and the heat will stay turned up on head coach Dan Quinn and GM Adam Peters.

Our second question asked which team respondents believe will win the NFC East division title in 2026. Despite Washington sitting behind both New York and Dallas in the standings, the results were fairly decisive:

That 54% vote represents a dramatic reversal from earlier in the season, but it stems less from sudden perfection in Ashburn than from the vulnerabilities of Washington's rivals.

First, Washington owns the head-to-head tiebreaker over the Giants (5-3-1) after Thursday's win. Second, the schedule-makers did New York no favors. Over the final 8 weeks, the Giants must run a gauntlet that includes Jacksonville, San Francisco, Seattle, and Detroit, whereas Washington gets much softer matchups against Arizona, Tennessee, and Atlanta.

Meanwhile, Dallas (4-6) has been unable to win back-to-back games since November 28, 2025, and its defensive struggles reached a boiling point this week when head coach Brian Schottenheimer stripped first-year coordinator Christian Parker of play-calling duties in favor of senior defensive assistant Mike Pettine. To beat out Washington for the division title, the Cowboys will have to win as many times in their remaining 7 games as the Commanders win in their final 8. Don't forget that the Commanders still have a chance to inflict a loss on the Cowboys when they meet in Landover for the final game of the regular season in Week 18.

The math is straightforward: if Washington can match the win totals of both the Giants and Cowboys, the Commanders will be the division winner!

Washington holds the tiebreaker over the Giants, and, because of the Week 5 tie, matching Dallas in the win column would put Washington a half-game ahead of the silver & blue.

If, for example, the Commanders can win 5 of their final 8 games to finish 8-8-1, they would be division champs if the Giants go 3-5 and the Cowboys, who have only 7 games remaining, finish out the season 4-3. Of course, with 8 weeks to go, there are sure to be a lot of twists & turns before things are decided, but the Cowboys' games in Week 17 vs the Giants and Week 18 on the road against the Commanders could prove to be decisive in determining the ultimate division champ.

Readers in the survey article comment thread broke cleanly along familiar lines.

The optimistic Pollyanna camp — voiced by Savo_UK, Redskins21Life, and BillWard among others– pointed directly to the schedule imbalance. Their consensus is that if Washington takes care of business against Arizona, Tennessee, Atlanta, and Dallas (in Week 18), stealing just 1 upset against Cincinnati, Houston, Minnesota, or Jacksonville would be enough to reach 8 wins, which could be enough to claim the division in this 2026 season.

The skeptical Cassandra camp — led by theFan4Life, WillThomas, and Greengmork among others — warned against mistaking a single prime-time escape against the Giants for a turnaround. They argue that Washington's defense still struggles to generate consistent pressure, that the roster lacks the talent to beat playoff-caliber offenses, and that finishing around .500 risks trapping the franchise in draft-position purgatory without building a true contender.

I think the Commanders defense has looked completely different since the return of Nick Cross and Trey Amos to the lineup.

In the first 5 games of 2026, the Commanders surrendered an average of 29.8 points per game. Since Week 6 in San Francisco, when Amos and Cross took the field together for the first time, Washington has given up 18.5 points per game. While that stretch did include games against the struggling Eagles and last week's road trip to New York, it also included games against the Niners and Rams.

While passing yards surrendered since Week 6 are not dramatically improved (255 per game in Wks 1-5; 232 per game in Wks 6-10), the number of passing TOUCHDOWNS surrendered has changed dramatically, from 2.8 per game (14 total) in the first 5 weeks to 0.75 per game (3 total) in the four most recent games; the secondary has not allowed more than one passing touchdown in any of those contests.

Week 1 at Eagles (L 24–22): 182 net yds (203 gross), 3 TDs

Week 2 at Cowboys (L 37–20): 266 net yds (279 gross), 4 TDs

Week 3 vs. Seahawks (W 33–31): 393 net yds (393 gross), 4 TDs

Week 4 vs. Colts (L 30–13): 129 net yds (143 gross), 0 TDs

Week 5 vs. Giants (T 27–27): 305 net yds (326 gross), 3 TDs

Week 6 at 49ers (L 23–17): 249 net yds (264 gross), 1 TD

Week 8 vs. Eagles (W 27–10): 177 net yds (212 gross), 0 TDs

Week 9 vs. Rams (L 20–13): 219 net yds (228 gross), 1 TD

Week 10 at Giants (W 24–21): 283 net yds (293 gross), 1 TD

Anyone looking at the full-season stats for Washington's pass defense would question the Commanders' ability to limit the Bengals' high-powered attack this week. But a look at the most recent 4 games — which include playing against Matt Stafford & Puka Nacua as well as Brock Purdy and the Kyle Shanahan offense — might well bolster the confidence of Commanders fans with respect to improvements in Washington's pass defense since Cross and Amos took the field together.

If you only look at the scoreboard, it is easy to argue that Washington's offense is still spinning its wheels. To my eye, though, the underlying efficiency and rhythm of David Blough's unit have taken a noticeable step forward over the past month.

Across the four-game stretch straddling the Week 7 bye, the Commanders have pushed their average to 7.6 yards per pass attempt—a marked jump from the 4.8 to 6.3 yards per attempt they managed during the opening month—while completing 66.7 percent of their throws. Even in the Week 9 loss to Los Angeles, Jayden Daniels posted a 101.4 passer rating (24-of-35 for 253 yards) with explosive strikes of 42 yards to Stefon Diggs, 21 yards to Van Jefferson, and a 45-yard burst from Rachaad White, before following that up on Thursday night in New York by going 23-of-33 for 235 yards and three touchdowns in a 24–21 road win over the Giants.

That progress reflects a deliberate coaching emphasis on sharper situational execution; prior to Week 10, head coach Dan Quinn noted that the staff added extra practice repetitions specifically targeting red-zone spacing against man and zone looks so that "the decision-making process happens quickly" and the offense stops settling for field goals.

Just as encouraging has been the growing trust between Daniels and his first-time play-caller, paired with Daniels' emergence as the unit's vocal leader. There were plenty of justifiable questions early on about Blough's learning curve, yet Daniels emphasized ahead of the Giants game that their weekly game-planning loop is actively tailoring the playbook to his strengths: "I think just stuff I don't like throughout the week, he'll take out. Stuff I really do like, he'll call… If we got all 11 people going in the same direction, we have a chance."

After some early-season failures, that buy-in has shown up recently when it has mattered, most notably during Week 8's 27–10 win over Philadelphia, when Daniels gathered the offense during a sluggish 10–0 start—calling their opening series "complete BS"—and sparked 27 unanswered points.

Rookie wideout Antonio Williams credited Daniels' command in the huddle for uniting the group ("All the guys respect what he has to say, because we know the work he puts in"), while Quinn praised the quarterback's resilience in Thursday night's comeback after overcoming a turnover on downs and two interceptions to engineer the game-winning drive, saying, "To see us battle and stay in the fight, that's what I was looking for."

All in all, I see a team that struggled early and is still finding its feet, but one that is on the rise. The Commanders still haven't put together that full 60-minute game that was the hallmark of the 2024 season, but I believe that they're moving in that direction, and, with 8 games left to play, there's plenty of time to finish putting it together.

Meanwhile, in a different SB Nation poll this week:

Outside the DMV, barely one in six voters (15.7%) wanted a piece of Washington at +350—a stark contrast to the 54% of Hogs Haven readers projecting a division crown, and a gap the Commanders can start closing under the lights on Monday night.

The final 8-game push begins Monday night at Northwest Stadium against Joe Burrow and the AFC North-leading Cincinnati Bengals.

Anyone who watched the last regular-season meeting between these clubs on Monday Night Football in September 2024 remembers a clinic in offensive efficiency. Washington won 38-33 in a game that featured zero punts and zero turnovers — a feat the NFL hadn't seen since 1940.

If Washington wants to get the win again this Monday, the Commanders may need to be ready for another track meet. Burrow has Cincinnati rolling on a five-game winning streak, averaging 30.9 points per game behind Ja'Marr Chase and Tee Higgins. For Washington to pull the upset, pass rushers Dorance Armstrong, Odafe Oweh, and K'Lavon Chaisson will have to disrupt Burrow's timing while the secondary continues to improve, and the Daniels-led offense keeps pace on the scoreboard.

Moneyline: Bengals -270 / Commanders +220

Winning on Monday night is a tall order, although, with Arizona and Tennessee up next, even a loss wouldn't derail Washington's plan for a division title. Still, catching fire under the Monday night lights would likely turn the current cautious optimism into genuine belief across the DMV.

Harry Kane's Bayern blow: England captain ruled out for MONTHS after tearing ankle ligaments - but Ballon d'Or winner sends upbeat message from hospital

📅 2026-11-20  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Published: 17:58 EST, 20 November 2026 | Updated: 06:31 EST, 21 November 2026

Bayern Munich fear Harry Kane will be ruled out for months after suffering an ankle injury in their Bundesliga draw against Cologne - with the striker revealing he will only return for 'the business end of the season'.

Kane was forced off just 20 minutes into the match at the Allianz Arena following a challenge from Joel Schmied.

The defender had won the ball but caught Kane with his follow through on his ankle.

Kane had initially tried to play on after treatment, before eventually needing to be replaced.

The Ballon d'Or winner was upbeat on Saturday morning however, sharing a message with his fans that stressed his readiness to come back better than ever.

'The highs and lows of life!' Kane captioned a picture of himself wearing a protective boot. 'Obviously very disappointed to get this injury but as always it's an opportunity to show resilience and improvement going in 2027.

Harry Kane was forced off during Bayern Munich's draw against Cologne on Friday night

Bayern confirmed the England captain is set to be out for months following the injury

But despite his injury, Kane was upbeat as he spoke to fans for the first time on Saturday (Source: Harry Kane/X)

'I'll be back fresh and ready to go for the business end of the season!

'"Tough times don't last, tough people do" - my guy (Julian Edelman) told me this one!' Kane added, quoting the NFL icon.

'Thank you for all the messages! Alles gut (red heart emoji, strong arm emoji).'

Speaking post-match, Bayern sporting director Max Eberl revealed that Kane has suffered ligament damage.

A Bayern club statement confirmed the blow, reading: 'Harry Kane suffered a tear of the syndesmosis ligament in his left ankle in Bayern Munich’s Bundesliga home match against 1. FC Köln on Friday evening.

'This was confirmed by an examination by Bayern Munich’s medical department.

'The 33-year-old will therefore be out for the next few months. Get well soon Harry.'

Kane's absence will be a major blow for Bayern, with the Ballon d'Or winner having scored ten goals in 16 matches so far this season.

Bayern, who are already without Luis Diaz, Saibari and Serge Gnabry, also saw Michael Olise come off at half-time.

The German giants later confirmed Olise had suffered a 'contusion of the forefoot', with the Frenchman set to undergo treatment in the coming days.

It remains to be seen whether Kane - who became England's most-capped player last week when making his 126th appearance in the 2-1 win over Croatia - is at risk of missing the Nations League quarter-final against Norway in March.

Vincent Kompany, who was back in the dugout for the first time since his collapse last month, admitted other players will have to step up for Bayern in the absence of star names such as Kane.

'These are world-class players, you can't replace them, but we have the mentality and we have to bring that now,' Kompany said, as per Bild.

'We need incredible performances from players who may not yet be used to this pressure at FC Bayern.

Bayern were held to a 1-1 draw by Cologne on Vincent Kompany's return to the touchline

'We will all try together to make this situation a positive one for ourselves. FC Bayern does this time and time again, and that's what we want to do now too.'

Bayern were ultimately held to a 1-1 draw by Cologne, with Tottenham loanee Mikey Moore giving the visitors a shock first half lead at the Allianz Arena.

Nathaniel Brown drew Bayern level before half-time, but the hosts were unable to find a winner on Kompany's return.

The draw gives Borussia Dortmund the chance to move to the top of the Bundesliga table on Saturday.
